Then exercise **persisted, topology-anchored annotations**: on `bull.stp`, take any measurement (e.g. Distance between two faces) → confirm a new **📌 Pin** button appears next to **⟟ Exact** in the readout row. Click it → confirm a status message confirms the pin and a new row appears in the **Saved** list at the bottom of the Measure ▾ panel, showing the frozen readout text. Click **Show** on it → confirm the overlay redisplays at the same position/text (no host round trip, no recompute). Close and reopen the tab → confirm the pinned annotation reloads from `bull.stp.annotations.json` (inspect it: valid JSON, `{version, source, annotations}`, CAD file untouched) and the Saved list repopulates. Apply an edit that renumbers ids elsewhere on the model (e.g. a fillet on an unrelated edge, mirroring the entity-rebinding test above) → confirm the pinned annotation's row is unaffected (not struck through) since its own anchor survived the rebind; then apply an edit that consumes/removes the SPECIFIC face/edge the annotation anchored to (e.g. a boolean that fuses it into another solid) → confirm the row goes struck-through with **Show** disabled (detached) rather than silently pointing at the wrong geometry. Delete it (✕) → confirm it's gone from both the panel and the sidecar. Take a measurement and Pin it on `cube.stl` too → confirm pinning works on a mesh source (unlike **⟟ Exact**, which stays B-rep only). Via the MCP server, call `get_state` on a document with a pinned annotation → confirm the response's `annotations` array reflects it; then `apply_edit_ops` a topology-changing op that affects its anchor → confirm the `warnings` array mentions rebinding/dropping the annotation anchor alongside any Part rebinding, and `get_state` afterward shows the updated (or emptied, if genuinely unresolvable) anchor ids. Repeat open/close + pin/delete a few times → watch extension-host/webview memory stay flat (same leak check as above).

Then exercise **archive integrity**: on `bull.stp` (with a Part, a pinned annotation, and an edit applied), run **File ▸ Save Preprocess…** to get a real `.zip` → unzip it by hand and confirm `manifest.json` now has `version: 2`, a `minimumReaderVersion`, and a `checksums` object listing every packaged entry (source + `.parts.json` + `.annotations.json` + `.edits.json`), and confirm there is **no** `.geo` file in the archive at all. Run **File ▸ Load Preprocess…** on the untampered archive → confirm it restores cleanly (parts, the pinned annotation, and edits all present in the reopened document) — same as before this feature. Now unzip a fresh copy, flip a single byte in the packaged `bull.stp` entry, re-zip it, and try to load THAT → confirm a clear checksum-mismatch error instead of a silent corrupt restore. Separately, hand-edit `manifest.json`'s `minimumReaderVersion` to `999` in an otherwise-untampered archive and try to load it → confirm a clear "requires a newer version of CAD Preview" error. Try restoring the (untampered) STEP archive by picking a destination filename ending in `.stl` in the Save-As dialog → confirm a clear "destination file extension doesn't match" error rather than a silently-wrong file; repeat picking `.step` (not `.stp`) instead → confirm it succeeds (same-format alias). Repeat the checksum-tamper, version-gate, and format-mismatch checks via the MCP server's `save_preprocess`/`load_preprocess` tools → confirm the same three rejections. Finally, hand-build a legacy pre-this-feature archive (a bare `{"version":1,"source":"bull.stp"}` manifest, no `checksums`/`minimumReaderVersion`) and load it → confirm it still opens without complaint (the migration path).

Then exercise **XCAF assembly structure**: open `examples/STP/as1_pe.stp` → confirm the Components tree shows a real nested hierarchy (an "Assembly N" group containing sub-groups and leaf "Solid N" rows) instead of a flat 12-item list — expand it and confirm clicking/hiding a LEAF row still highlights/hides that solid exactly as before (group-header rows are inert for both, a known accepted limitation). Open `examples/STP/bull.stp` → confirm it now shows a single "Assembly 1" wrapper around its one solid (it's genuinely a small 2-part assembly, not an unstructured single body) — assign a Part to a face, apply a Fillet elsewhere, confirm entity-id rebinding still works exactly as documented above (this feature never touches `solid-N`/`face-N`/`edge-N` ids, only how they're grouped for display). Open `examples/STP/angle1.stp` (a genuinely flat, non-assembly file) → confirm the tree is unchanged from before this feature (a single flat "Solid 1" row, no "Assembly" wrapper). On `as1_pe.stp`, add a **Box** primitive (a topology-changing edit) → confirm the tree gracefully falls back to a flat list including the new solid (the cached assembly structure can no longer form a clean bijection against the changed solid count) rather than showing a stale or broken hierarchy; Undo the box → confirm the nested assembly tree reappears. Open a `.iges`/`.brep`/mesh-format file → confirm the tree is completely unaffected (this feature is STEP-only). Time opening `as1_pe.stp`/a large STEP fixture and confirm it's noticeably but not dramatically slower than before (roughly 2x load time is expected and accepted — see CLAUDE.md's own perf-cost note above; run `npm run perf` for a precise before/after if in doubt). Repeat open/close and edit/undo a few times → watch extension-host memory stay flat (same OCCT-handle leak check as every other feature above — this one specifically exercises whether the SECOND parse's handles are being freed correctly, not just the primary read's).

Then exercise **XCAF write**: on `bull.stp`, assign two Parts with distinct names to two different solids (add a Box primitive first if you want a guaranteed second solid) → Export to STEP → confirm the export succeeds with no warnings. Reopen the exported file → confirm both solids are still present (the read-side fallback for this build's AP242 "document management" quirk kicks in transparently — there's nothing to see except that it just works) and their geometry/volume matches the original. Open the exported `.step` in a text editor and confirm the `PRODUCT` entities carry your part names, not generic placeholders. Export the SAME model with no Parts assigned at all → confirm the output has no `DOCUMENT_FILE` text (plain writer, unaffected). Confirm colors are NOT carried over (expected — investigated and confirmed non-functional in this OCCT build, not a bug).

Then exercise the **Standard Parts panel**: type a query (e.g. "M6 hex bolt") into the new Standard Parts sidebar section and click Search → confirm a results list appears with names/descriptions/categories (or a clear "unreachable" status if the step.parts API isn't reachable from this environment — that's a legitimate, expected outcome, not a bug). Click Insert on a result → confirm a save dialog appears defaulting to `<part-id>.step` next to the current document; save it → confirm the file downloads, opens as a new tab, and the panel's status line shows the saved path. Click Insert again and cancel the save dialog → confirm the button re-enables with no error toast (a dismissed dialog is a quiet no-op, not a failure).

Then exercise **align and pattern**: on `bull.stp`, add a Box primitive, select it (Vol mode), open **Align** in the Edits panel's Assembly category, set axis=z/extent=min/to=0, Apply → confirm the box's bottom face lands exactly on z=0. Select it again, open **Linear Pattern**, set direction/spacing/count=4, Apply → confirm 3 new evenly-spaced copies appear (4 total instances) and the Components tree shows the new solids. Repeat with **Circular Pattern** (axisPoint/axisDir/angleDeg/count) → confirm the copies arc around the axis at equal angular spacing, all at the same radius. Undo → confirm the pattern's new solids disappear cleanly. Close and reopen the tab → confirm all four ops (align + both patterns) reload correctly from `bull.stp.edits.json`. Repeat on `cube.stl` (mesh source) → confirm align/pattern work identically via the Three.js engine (both are NOT B-rep only).

Then exercise the **Transform Gizmo** (this is the one feature in this session that genuinely needs a real F5 pass — the automated checks above only cover its pure delta math, not the actual mouse-drag interaction): on `bull.stp`, select a solid (Vol mode), open **Translate** in the Edits panel → confirm a 3-axis drag gizmo appears at the solid's centre. Drag the X arrow → confirm the solid moves live along X ONLY, the Translate form's `Δ` field updates in real time to match, and releasing the mouse leaves the preview showing (no op pushed yet — check the history list is unchanged). Click Apply → confirm the previewed position becomes the real committed edit. Switch to **Rotate** → confirm a set of rotation rings appears; drag one → confirm the solid rotates about the selection's centroid (not the world origin, unless the centroid happens to be there), and that `axisPoint`/`axisDir`/`angleDeg` all update live. Switch to **Scale** → confirm drag handles appear and dragging scales the solid, updating `center`/`factors`. Select TWO solids at once (shift-click) and try each mode → confirm both move/rotate/scale together as one rigid group about their shared bbox centroid, not independently. Mid-drag, confirm orbit/pan is suspended (dragging the gizmo never also spins the camera) and that releasing the drag restores normal orbit. Click on a DIFFERENT solid while a drag preview is showing (uncommitted) → confirm the gizmo re-attaches to the new selection and the OLD solid's preview is discarded (snaps back to its original position, not left stranded). Switch to a non-transform form (e.g. Mate) → confirm the gizmo disappears and any uncommitted preview resets. Apply/undo/switch forms repeatedly → watch webview memory stay flat (same leak-check discipline as every other feature above).

Then exercise **grid and entity snapping**: on `bull.stp`, open **View ▾** → confirm **Snap to grid** and **Snap to points** both start unchecked, and the Appearance group's **Grid size** field defaults to `1`. Check **Snap to grid**, select a solid (Vol mode), open **Translate**, and drag the X arrow slowly → confirm the solid's position jumps in discrete 1-unit increments rather than moving continuously; change **Grid size** to e.g. `5` mid-drag (release, reopen the drag) → confirm the increment changes to 5. Uncheck grid snap, check **Snap to points** instead, and drag a solid so its dragged corner passes near an existing vertex on another part → confirm it snaps exactly onto that point when close enough, and moves freely again once dragged well past it. Enable BOTH, select two solids at once, and drag → confirm they move together as a rigid group when neither is near a snap point, but confirm it's OK (expected, documented behavior) if one target snaps to a nearby point while the other doesn't — they're allowed to diverge slightly in that case. Apply a snapped drag → confirm the committed op's numbers match what was previewed. Toggle both snap modes off → confirm dragging returns to fully free movement.

Then exercise **SVG import**: create a small test SVG with a simple traced shape (e.g. export a rectangle or a circle from Inkscape/Illustrator as plain `<path>` data, or hand-write one with a few `L`/`C`/`A` commands). On `bull.stp`, **File ▾ → Import SVG…** → pick the file → confirm a status line reports how many paths were imported, and that new `addPolyline` entries appear in the Edits history (one per subpath). Switch to **Line** pick mode and confirm the imported polyline's edges are selectable/highlightable like any other wireframe entity. Select a closed imported polyline in Line mode and **Build → Surface** → confirm it builds a flat sketch face (under "Sketches"), then select it in Surf mode and **Extrude** → confirm it builds a real solid. Try an SVG containing a shape with a hole (e.g. a path with two subpaths, like a traced letter "O" or a rectangle-with-rectangle-hole) → confirm TWO separate polylines import, each independently selectable. Import onto `cube.stl` (a mesh source) → confirm a clear "B-rep only" status message instead of a silent no-op. Cancel the file-picker dialog → confirm nothing happens (no error, no op pushed). Close and reopen the tab → confirm the imported `addPolyline` ops reload correctly from the sidecar, same as any other edit op.

Then exercise **progress reporting and cancellation**: open a large B-rep fixture (e.g. `examples/STP/turbine.stp`) fresh → confirm a native VS Code notification (bottom-right) appears titled "CAD Preview: Loading STEP…" with a Cancel button, its message text stepping through "Loading STEP kernel…" → "Tessellating STEP…" → "Rendering…", and disappearing once the model displays; confirm the webview's own toolbar status line shows the same three messages in parallel. Reopen the same file and click **Cancel** on the notification partway through → confirm the toolbar status immediately shows "Cancelled", the notification dismisses, and — once the (uninterrupted, still-running) background computation actually finishes a moment later — nothing further happens (no stale geometry appears, no error). Now open a SMALL fixture (e.g. `angle1.stp`) and apply several quick successive edits (e.g. add 3 primitives back-to-back without waiting) → confirm NO native notification pops up for any of them (routine edits stay on the lightweight status line only, per this feature's deliberate scoping) and the final displayed geometry reflects the LAST edit applied, not an intermediate one (the stale-result-discard fix — try deliberately spamming edits fast to stress this). Trigger the external-file-change-reload path (edit the CAD source from a separate terminal while the tab is open) → confirm a notification appears for that path too (the second `showProgress` call site). Repeat open/close and cancel a few times → watch extension-host memory stay flat (same leak check as above — a cancelled load's OCCT handles must still be freed normally once the background computation completes, not leaked).

Then exercise the **kernel-worker child process (MCP server side)**: run `npm run mcp:smoke` (must pass, all 154 checks, unchanged) and `npm run perf` (compare against `scripts/perf/baseline.json` — expect only a small, single-digit-percent overhead from the added IPC hop, not a regression) — both already build `dist/kernel-worker.js` as part of `npm run build`. For a hands-on fault-isolation check: register the server (`claude mcp add cad-preview -- node $PWD/dist/mcp-server.js`), have an agent start a deliberately slow `generate_mesh` call (a fine `sizeMax` on a large fixture like `turbine.stp`), then from a separate terminal find the spawned child (`pgrep -P <mcp-server.js pid>`, confirm via `ps -o args= -p <pid>` that it's `dist/kernel-worker.js`) and kill it (`kill -9 <pid>`) — confirm the in-flight tool call returns a clear error (`"kernel worker exited unexpectedly..."`) rather than hanging forever, that the MCP server process itself is still alive (`kill -0 <server pid>`), and that the agent's very next tool call (e.g. `load_model`) succeeds normally. `dist/kernel-worker.js`'s own stderr output (prefixed nowhere on its own, since it inherits the parent's stderr via the pipe-and-forward wiring — look for the `[kernel-worker]`-prefixed lines `mcp-server.js`'s own stderr shows) should contain only expected diagnostic output (OCCT's Emscripten banner, Gmsh's `Info :`/`Warning :` lines), never anything on its stdout (structurally impossible to observe either way, since it's spawned with stdout `"ignore"`).

Then exercise the **kernel-worker child process (interactive extension side, Phase 2+3) — this is the one genuinely unverified piece from this session, since no real Extension Development Host session was run here.** Open a B-rep file (`bull.stp`) and confirm it loads normally through the new document-cache path (the progress notification still shows, the model still renders correctly). Apply several edits in a row — confirm each re-tessellates correctly and that later edits feel noticeably faster than the first (the same base-shape-cache reuse the closed "Base-shape caching" feature always provided, now living inside the child instead of in-process — should be indistinguishable in behavior, only in *where* the cache lives). Open a SECOND B-rep file in another tab, edit both back and forth — confirm neither document's geometry/cache is affected by the other's edits (independent `documentKey`s). Start a genuinely slow load (a fine tessellation quality setting on a large file) and click **Cancel** on the progress notification — confirm the toolbar status shows "Cancelled" promptly (not after the full parse completes) and that a subsequent edit on the SAME document still works correctly afterward (the shared child respawning transparently). Close a document's tab, then verify (via `ps aux | grep kernel-worker` while the extension host is still running, or by triggering another document's edit and confirming the closed document's cache doesn't linger meaningfully) that its cache was released rather than accumulating forever across many open/close cycles — a long F5 session opening and closing many CAD files should NOT show ever-growing memory in the kernel-worker process. Confirm the whole extension still behaves normally with ZERO documents open (the child is spawned lazily on the first real kernel call, not at activation) and that quitting VS Code entirely leaves no orphaned `kernel-worker.js` process behind (`ps aux | grep kernel-worker` after quitting — the child is not spawned `detached`, so it should die with the extension host automatically).

Then exercise the **Mesh Health panel** (roadmap "Mesh → B-rep promotion", both phases — this is the one genuinely unverified piece from this session's work, since no real Extension Development Host session was run here): open `examples/STL/cube.stl` → confirm a new **Mesh Health** section appears in the sidebar (below Mass Properties) with **Check Healability** and (disabled) **Promote to B-rep…** buttons; click Check Healability → confirm it shows "Checking…" then a single component row with `0` free/non-manifold edges, `0` degenerate faces, a required tolerance around `1e-6`, and area/volume deltas near `0%`, and confirm **Promote to B-rep…** is now enabled. Click **Promote to B-rep…** → confirm a format quick-pick (STEP/IGES/BREP), then a unit quick-pick (defaulting to native mm), then a save dialog appear in sequence; save as STEP → confirm the toolbar status shows "Exported to …" and the CAD Preview: Open the new file → confirm it opens as an ordinary B-rep document with the correct geometry (same bounding box/volume as the original cube), and that fillet/chamfer/Mass Properties/further Export all work on it normally; confirm `cube.stl` itself is byte-identical to before (untouched). Open `examples/GLTF/cube.gltf` **and `examples/GLTF/cube.glb`** → confirm the Mesh Health section appears for both (glTF gained a host-side parser), Check Healability reports 1 component closing at `1e-6` with ~0 deltas, and **Promote to B-rep…** writes a STEP that reopens with volume 1. Also confirm that opening a `.gltf` still does NOT load the OCCT WASM (extension-host memory stays flat) — the lazy-WASM invariant is unaffected, since the mesh parsers are pure JS. Open `bull.stp` (a B-rep source) → confirm the Mesh Health section is entirely hidden. Open `examples/OBJ/cube.obj` and `examples/PLY/cube.ply` → confirm the panel appears, Check Healability works for both, and Promote produces a correct STEP file for each too. Open a meshio-imported document (e.g. a `.med`/`.vtk` file, per the meshio++ integration section above) → confirm the panel stays hidden even though the document displays as an ordinary triangle mesh (its underlying bytes are a converted STL boundary, not a real `.stl`/`.obj`/`.ply` file on disk — same gate `check_mesh_health`/`promote_mesh_to_brep`'s MCP tools apply to the real file path). (A meshio-converted document stays ineligible — its bytes are a converted STL boundary, not a real mesh file on disk.) Switch between an eligible and an ineligible document a few times → confirm the panel's visibility and its body content both track the currently-focused tab correctly, with no stale report left showing from a previously-viewed file, and confirm **Promote to B-rep…** starts disabled again on a freshly-opened eligible document until Check Healability has been run.

On **VS Code Remote/SSH**, the running extension is the installed copy in `~/.vscode-server/extensions/`, not the workspace `dist/` — rebuilds alone won't show up. Bump the version, `npx vsce package`, reinstall the `.vsix`, then reload the window.
